========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… um, I came in because my mind just won’t stop. It keeps jumping from one worry to another, and I can’t seem to get it to settle down. Like, I work in a lab, and there’s this new partnership happening, and I keep thinking, what if that means they’re going to cut my position? And then even if I try to focus on work, I start worrying that my data are wrong or that I missed something obvious and everyone’s going to find out.

And then it kind of spirals into everything else—bills, whether they’re going to pile up, whether my family thinks I’m falling behind compared to where I should be, and whether I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school. It’s like my brain keeps moving through all these worst-case scenarios, one after another, and I can’t really shut it off.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It feels pretty constant, honestly. Like, it definitely gets louder when something changes or there’s a specific thing happening, but it doesn’t need that to start. I can be trying to do something very normal, like finish a report or check an assay, and I’ll tell myself, okay, just focus on this one task, just get through this one thing. But then one worry sets off another one, and then another, and before I know it I’m just staring at the screen, not really reading what’s in front of me, because my head is already running through every possible way things could go wrong.

And it’s not like I’m choosing to think about it. I’ll try to reason with myself, like, “You don’t have evidence for that,” or “You can deal with it later,” but it doesn’t really stop. It’s more like the worry just keeps branching out on its own, and I’m kind of stuck following it.

Yeah, the restlessness is probably the biggest one I notice. At work especially, I feel kind of wound up, like I can’t just sit in my chair and do the thing in front of me. I’ll be trying to analyze something or write notes, and I’m tapping my foot the whole time, or shifting around, or checking my messages even when I know there’s probably nothing there.

And I keep getting up for no real reason. Like I’ll walk back to the instrument room, and then once I’m there I’m like, why did I come in here? I don’t actually need to check anything right now. But sitting still feels… I don’t know, almost impossible sometimes. And I get jumpy too—normal lab noises, like something clicking on or someone closing a drawer, can startle me way more than they should. It’s embarrassing because it makes me feel like I’m on edge all day.

    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I don’t think I’m, like, hopeless exactly. I wouldn’t say that’s the main thing. It’s more that I’m so caught up in my head that I don’t always have much room for other things, if that makes sense.

Like with hobbies or seeing people, I don’t know that I’ve lost interest in them in the sense that I don’t care anymore. It’s more like when I’m doing them, I’m not fully there. I might go along with something or talk to family, but part of me is still kind of mentally elsewhere, thinking through things. So I guess I still want to enjoy stuff, and I do sometimes, but it can feel harder to actually settle into it.

But no, I don’t think I’ve been walking around feeling hopeless or like nothing matters. It’s more… tense and preoccupied, I guess.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… yes, most days it feels really hard to control. It’s not always about something big, either. Sometimes it’s something totally normal or small, like finishing a report, checking an assay, making sure I didn’t miss something obvious, and I’ll tell myself, just focus on this one task, just do the next step.

But then it’s like one worry opens the door to another one. I’ll think, what if I made a mistake here, and then it turns into what if that affects the results, and then what if someone notices, or what if I don’t catch it in time, and then suddenly I’m not even really doing the thing anymore. I’m just staring at the screen, kind of frozen, while my head is running through every possible way it could go wrong.

And I do try to stop it. I’ll try to be logical with myself or redirect back to what I’m supposed to be doing, but it doesn’t feel like something I can just switch off. It keeps going even when I know it’s not helping.

Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah. It’s definitely not just one specific problem. That’s actually kind of why I came in, because it feels like my brain won’t stay on one worry long enough for me to even deal with it.

Like, I’ll start with work — whether this new partnership our lab has is going to mean cuts, and whether my position is going to be one of the ones they decide they don’t need. And then before I’ve even finished thinking that through, I’m worried my data are wrong, or that I missed something in the analysis, or that someone’s going to find some mistake I should’ve caught.

Then it jumps to money, like whether my bills are going to start piling up if anything changes at work, or even if nothing changes and I’m just not managing things well enough. And then it turns into my family thinking I’m falling behind, or that I’m not doing enough with my life. And then grad school — like maybe I’ve already messed up my chances somehow, or I waited too long, or I haven’t done enough to be taken seriously.

So… yeah, it’s a lot of different things. It’s not like there’s one clear emergency I can point to. It’s more like everything feels like it could become a problem, and my mind keeps jumping between all of it most days.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n6\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F43.22, F33.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. I'm Dr. [Name]. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… um, I came in because my mind just won’t stop. It keeps jumping from one worry to another, and I can’t seem to get it to settle down. Like, I work in a lab, and there’s this new partnership happening, and I keep thinking, what if that means they’re going to cut my position? And then even if I try to focus on work, I start worrying that my data are wrong or that I missed something obvious and everyone’s going to find out.\n\nAnd then it kind of spirals into everything else—bills, whether they’re going to pile up, whether my family thinks I’m falling behind compared to where I should be, and whether I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school. It’s like my brain keeps moving through all these worst-case scenarios, one after another, and I can’t really shut it off.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries feel constant and uncontrollable, even when there isn’t a specific stressor happening, or do they mostly come up in response to certain situations or changes in your life?\n\nPatient: It feels pretty constant, honestly. Like, it definitely gets louder when something changes or there’s a specific thing happening, but it doesn’t need that to start. I can be trying to do something very normal, like finish a report or check an assay, and I’ll tell myself, okay, just focus on this one task, just get through this one thing. But then one worry sets off another one, and then another, and before I know it I’m just staring at the screen, not really reading what’s in front of me, because my head is already running through every possible way things could go wrong.\n\nAnd it’s not like I’m choosing to think about it. I’ll try to reason with myself, like, “You don’t have evidence for that,” or “You can deal with it later,” but it doesn’t really stop. It’s more like the worry just keeps branching out on its own, and I’m kind of stuck following it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms along with these worries, like feeling restless, being easily fatigued, having trouble sleeping, or muscle tension?\n\nPatient: Yeah, the restlessness is probably the biggest one I notice. At work especially, I feel kind of wound up, like I can’t just sit in my chair and do the thing in front of me. I’ll be trying to analyze something or write notes, and I’m tapping my foot the whole time, or shifting around, or checking my messages even when I know there’s probably nothing there.\n\nAnd I keep getting up for no real reason. Like I’ll walk back to the instrument room, and then once I’m there I’m like, why did I come in here? I don’t actually need to check anything right now. But sitting still feels… I don’t know, almost impossible sometimes. And I get jumpy too—normal lab noises, like something clicking on or someone closing a drawer, can startle me way more than they should. It’s embarrassing because it makes me feel like I’m on edge all day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, like hobbies or time with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I don’t think I’m, like, hopeless exactly. I wouldn’t say that’s the main thing. It’s more that I’m so caught up in my head that I don’t always have much room for other things, if that makes sense.\n\nLike with hobbies or seeing people, I don’t know that I’ve lost interest in them in the sense that I don’t care anymore. It’s more like when I’m doing them, I’m not fully there. I might go along with something or talk to family, but part of me is still kind of mentally elsewhere, thinking through things. So I guess I still want to enjoy stuff, and I do sometimes, but it can feel harder to actually settle into it.\n\nBut no, I don’t think I’ve been walking around feeling hopeless or like nothing matters. It’s more… tense and preoccupied, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you worry constantly, even about small or everyday things, and does it feel difficult to control this worry most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, most days it feels really hard to control. It’s not always about something big, either. Sometimes it’s something totally normal or small, like finishing a report, checking an assay, making sure I didn’t miss something obvious, and I’ll tell myself, just focus on this one task, just do the next step.\n\nBut then it’s like one worry opens the door to another one. I’ll think, what if I made a mistake here, and then it turns into what if that affects the results, and then what if someone notices, or what if I don’t catch it in time, and then suddenly I’m not even really doing the thing anymore. I’m just staring at the screen, kind of frozen, while my head is running through every possible way it could go wrong.\n\nAnd I do try to stop it. I’ll try to be logical with myself or redirect back to what I’m supposed to be doing, but it doesn’t feel like something I can just switch off. It keeps going even when I know it’s not helping.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you feel anxious or worried about a number of different things, not just specific problems, and that this anxiety is present more days than not?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It’s definitely not just one specific problem. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure how to answer the “nearly every day” part. I wouldn’t say I’ve been feeling hopeless like that every day, or that I just don’t care about anything anymore. It’s not that clean for me.

There are times where things don’t feel as enjoyable as they probably should, but I don’t know if that’s the same as having little interest or pleasure. Like, I may still want to do things, but I’m not always fully able to relax into them. So if you’re asking whether I’ve been depressed or hopeless almost every day for the last two weeks, I don’t think that’s really it. But if you’re asking whether I’ve felt kind of mentally weighed down or not fully present, then… maybe, yeah, somewhat.

That’s actually kind of why I came in, because it feels like my brain won’t stay on one worry long enough for me to even deal with it.\n\nLike, I’ll start with work — whether this new partnership our lab has is going to mean cuts, and whether my position is going to be one of the ones they decide they don’t need. And then before I’ve even finished thinking that through, I’m worried my data are wrong, or that I missed something in the analysis, or that someone’s going to find some mistake I should’ve caught.\n\nThen it jumps to money, like whether my bills are going to start piling up if anything changes at work, or even if nothing changes and I’m just not managing things well enough. And then it turns into my family thinking I’m falling behind, or that I’m not doing enough with my life. And then grad school — like maybe I’ve already messed up my chances somehow, or I waited too long, or I haven’t done enough to be taken seriously.\n\nSo… yeah, it’s a lot of different things. It’s not like there’s one clear emergency I can point to. It’s more like everything feels like it could become a problem, and my mind keeps jumping between all of it most days.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you had little pleasure or interest in doing things, or felt down, depressed, or hopeless nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer the “nearly every day” part. I wouldn’t say I’ve been feeling hopeless like that every day, or that I just don’t care about anything anymore. It’s not that clean for me.\n\nThere are times where things don’t feel as enjoyable as they probably should, but I don’t know if that’s the same as having little interest or pleasure. Like, I may still want to do things, but I’m not always fully able to relax into them. So if you’re asking whether I’ve been depressed or hopeless almost every day for the last two weeks, I don’t think that’s really it. But if you’re asking whether I’ve felt kind of mentally weighed down or not fully present, then… maybe, yeah, somewhat.\n\n[Last Question]\nOver the past two weeks, have you felt down, depressed, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy for most days?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 24 / chemist_or_materials_scientist / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess I’m still not totally sure how to separate that from just being worn out and preoccupied. Like, I wouldn’t say I wake up most days thinking, “I’m depressed,” or that I don’t enjoy anything anymore. It’s more that if I try to do something I usually like, part of me is still somewhere else, if that makes sense.

So… for most days, maybe I’ve felt kind of muted or distracted, but I don’t know if I’d call it feeling down or hopeless. I haven’t really been tracking it that way. I notice more that I’m not as relaxed as I used to be, and that makes it harder to enjoy things fully, but I don’t think I’ve completely lost interest in them.

I notice more that I’m not as relaxed as I used to be, and that makes it harder to enjoy things fully, but I don’t think I’ve completely lost interest in them.\n\n[Last Question]\nOver the past two weeks, have you had days where you felt a loss of pleasure or interest in most activities you usually enjoy, even things that normally make you happy?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item.   {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Generalized Anxiety Disorder, in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you find that you feel anxious or worried about a number of different things, not just specific problems, and that this anxiety is present more days than not?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It’s definitely not just one specific problem. That’s actually kind of why I came in, because it feels like my brain won’t stay on one worry long enough for me to even deal with it.\n\nLike, I’ll start with work — whether this new partnership our lab has is going to mean cuts, and whether my position is going to be one of the ones they decide they don’t need. And then before I’ve even finished thinking that through, I’m worried my data are wrong, or that I missed something in the analysis, or that someone’s going to find some mistake I should’ve caught.\n\nThen it jumps to money, like whether my bills are going to start piling up if anything changes at work, or even if nothing changes and I’m just not managing things well enough. And then it turns into my family thinking I’m falling behind, or that I’m not doing enough with my life. And then grad school — like maybe I’ve already messed up my chances somehow, or I waited too long, or I haven’t done enough to be taken seriously.\n\nSo… yeah, it’s a lot of different things. It’s not like there’s one clear emergency I can point to. It’s more like everything feels like it could become a problem, and my mind keeps jumping between all of it most days.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you had little pleasure or interest in doing things, or felt down, depressed, or hopeless nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer the “nearly every day” part. I wouldn’t say I’ve been feeling hopeless like that every day, or that I just don’t care about anything anymore. It’s not that clean for me.\n\nThere are times where things don’t feel as enjoyable as they probably should, but I don’t know if that’s the same as having little interest or pleasure. Like, I may still want to do things, but I’m not always fully able to relax into them. So if you’re asking whether I’ve been depressed or hopeless almost every day for the last two weeks, I don’t think that’s really it. But if you’re asking whether I’ve felt kind of mentally weighed down or not fully present, then… maybe, yeah, somewhat.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt down, depressed, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy for most days?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m still not totally sure how to separate that from just being worn out and preoccupied. Like, I wouldn’t say I wake up most days thinking, “I’m depressed,” or that I don’t enjoy anything anymore. It’s more that if I try to do something I usually like, part of me is still somewhere else, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… for most days, maybe I’ve felt kind of muted or distracted, but I don’t know if I’d call it feeling down or hopeless. I haven’t really been tracking it that way. I notice more that I’m not as relaxed as I used to be, and that makes it harder to enjoy things fully, but I don’t think I’ve completely lost interest in them.\n\n[Last Question]\nOver the past two weeks, have you had days where you felt a loss of pleasure or interest in most activities you usually enjoy, even things that normally make you happy?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 24 / chemist_or_materials_scientist / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d say “most activities,” exactly. That sounds stronger than what I’m sure about. There have definitely been days where things that usually feel good don’t really land the same way, like I’m doing them but not really getting the normal enjoyment out of them.

But I’m hesitant to say it’s a full loss of pleasure, because sometimes I still want to do those things, or I can tell that some part of me would enjoy them if I could just… settle into it. It’s more like the enjoyment feels dulled or blocked, not completely gone. And I’m not sure if that counts as losing interest, because the interest is still sort of there, it’s just harder to access.

So, yes, there have been days like that, but I don’t know if I can honestly say it’s been most activities or that even happy things don’t make me happy at all. It’s more inconsistent and kind of hard to separate from being distracted or preoccupied.

And then even if I try to focus on work, I start worrying that my data are wrong or that I missed something obvious and everyone’s going to find out.\n\nAnd then it kind of spirals into everything else—bills, whether they’re going to pile up, whether my family thinks I’m falling behind compared to where I should be, and whether I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school. It’s like my brain keeps moving through all these worst-case scenarios, one after another, and I can’t really shut it off.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries feel constant and uncontrollable, even when there isn’t a specific stressor happening, or do they mostly come up in response to certain situations or changes in your life?\n\nPatient: It feels pretty constant, honestly. Like, it definitely gets louder when something changes or there’s a specific thing happening, but it doesn’t need that to start. I can be trying to do something very normal, like finish a report or check an assay, and I’ll tell myself, okay, just focus on this one task, just get through this one thing. But then one worry sets off another one, and then another, and before I know it I’m just staring at the screen, not really reading what’s in front of me, because my head is already running through every possible way things could go wrong.\n\nAnd it’s not like I’m choosing to think about it. I’ll try to reason with myself, like, “You don’t have evidence for that,” or “You can deal with it later,” but it doesn’t really stop. It’s more like the worry just keeps branching out on its own, and I’m kind of stuck following it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms along with these worries, like feeling restless, being easily fatigued, having trouble sleeping, or muscle tension?\n\nPatient: Yeah, the restlessness is probably the biggest one I notice. I don’t think I’m, like, hopeless exactly. I wouldn’t say that’s the main thing. It’s more that I’m so caught up in my head that I don’t always have much room for other things, if that makes sense.\n\nLike with hobbies or seeing people, I don’t know that I’ve lost interest in them in the sense that I don’t care anymore. It’s more like when I’m doing them, I’m not fully there. I might go along with something or talk to family, but part of me is still kind of mentally elsewhere, thinking through things. It’s more… tense and preoccupied, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you worry constantly, even about small or everyday things, and does it feel difficult to control this worry most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, most days it feels really hard to control. Yeah… yes, I think that’s actually a better description of what’s been going on than the depression questions. It’s not always the exact same worry, but it feels like my brain just keeps swapping one thing out for another.

Like, I came in because my mind is constantly jumping from whether our lab’s new partnership is going to mean my position gets cut, to whether the data I’ve been working with are wrong, to whether my bills are going to start piling up if something happens with work. Then it’ll shift to whether my family thinks I’m falling behind compared to where I should be, and then suddenly I’m thinking I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school somehow.

It’s not like I sit down and decide to worry about these things. It just keeps moving. Even if I talk myself down from one thing, another one kind of takes its place. So, more days than not, yeah, I’d say the worry is there. The topic changes, but the feeling doesn’t really stop.

Like, it definitely gets louder when something changes or there’s a specific thing happening, but it doesn’t need that to start. I can be trying to do something very normal, like finish a report or check an assay, and I’ll tell myself, okay, just focus on this one task, just get through this one thing. I’ll try to reason with myself, like, “You don’t have evidence for that,” or “You can deal with it later,” but it doesn’t really stop. It’s more like the worry just keeps branching out on its own, and I’m kind of stuck following it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms along with these worries, like feeling restless, being easily fatigued, having trouble sleeping, or muscle tension?\n\nPatient: Yeah, the restlessness is probably the biggest one I notice. At work especially, I feel kind of wound up, like I can’t just sit in my chair and do the thing in front of me. I’ll be trying to analyze something or write notes, and I’m tapping my foot the whole time, or shifting around, or checking my messages even when I know there’s probably nothing there.\n\nAnd I keep getting up for no real reason. Like I’ll walk back to the instrument room, and then once I’m there I’m like, why did I come in here? I don’t actually need to check anything right now. And I get jumpy too—normal lab noises, like something clicking on or someone closing a drawer, can startle me way more than they should. It’s embarrassing because it makes me feel like I’m on edge all day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, like hobbies or time with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I don’t think I’m, like, hopeless exactly. I wouldn’t say that’s the main thing. It’s more that I’m so caught up in my head that I don’t always have much room for other things, if that makes sense.\n\nLike with hobbies or seeing people, I don’t know that I’ve lost interest in them in the sense that I don’t care anymore. It’s more like when I’m doing them, I’m not fully there. I might go along with something or talk to family, but part of me is still kind of mentally elsewhere, thinking through things. So I guess I still want to enjoy stuff, and I do sometimes, but it can feel harder to actually settle into it.\n\nBut no, I don’t think I’ve been walking around feeling hopeless or like nothing matters. It’s more… tense and preoccupied, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you worry constantly, even about small or everyday things, and does it feel difficult to control this worry most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, most days it feels really hard to control. It’s not always about something big, either. Sometimes it’s something totally normal or small, like finishing a report, checking an assay, making sure I didn’t miss something obvious, and I’ll tell myself, just focus on this one task, just do the next step.\n\nBut then it’s like one worry opens the door to another one. I’ll think, what if I made a mistake here, and then it turns into what if that affects the results, and then what if someone notices, or what if I don’t catch it in time, and then suddenly I’m not even really doing the thing anymore. I’m just staring at the screen, kind of frozen, while my head is running through every possible way it could go wrong.\n\nAnd I do try to stop it. I’ll try to be logical with myself or redirect back to what I’m supposed to be doing, but it doesn’t feel like something I can just switch off. It keeps going even when I know it’s not helping.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you feel anxious or worried about a number of different things, not just specific problems, and that this anxiety is present more days than not?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It’s definitely not just one specific problem. That’s actually kind of why I came in, because it feels like my brain won’t stay on one worry long enough for me to even deal with it.\n\nLike, I’ll start with work — whether this new partnership our lab has is going to mean cuts, and whether my position is going to be one of the ones they decide they don’t need. And then before I’ve even finished thinking that through, I’m worried my data are wrong, or that I missed something in the analysis, or that someone’s going to find some mistake I should’ve caught.\n\nThen it jumps to money, like whether my bills are going to start piling up if anything changes at work, or even if nothing changes and I’m just not managing things well enough. And then it turns into my family thinking I’m falling behind, or that I’m not doing enough with my life. And then grad school — like maybe I’ve already messed up my chances somehow, or I waited too long, or I haven’t done enough to be taken seriously.\n\nSo… yeah, it’s a lot of different things. I wouldn’t say I’ve been feeling hopeless like that every day, or that I just don’t care about anything anymore. I’ll think, what if I made a mistake here, and then it turns into what if that affects the results, and then what if someone notices, or what if I don’t catch it in time, and then suddenly I’m not even really doing the thing anymore. I’m just staring at the screen, kind of frozen, while my head is running through every possible way it could go wrong.\n\nAnd I do try to stop it. I’ll try to be logical with myself or redirect back to what I’m supposed to be doing, but it doesn’t feel like something I can just switch off. It keeps going even when I know it’s not helping.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you feel anxious or worried about a number of different things, not just specific problems, and that this anxiety is present more days than not?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It’s definitely not just one specific problem. That’s actually kind of why I came in, because it feels like my brain won’t stay on one worry long enough for me to even deal with it.\n\nLike, I’ll start with work — whether this new partnership our lab has is going to mean cuts, and whether my position is going to be one of the ones they decide they don’t need. And then before I’ve even finished thinking that through, I’m worried my data are wrong, or that I missed something in the analysis, or that someone’s going to find some mistake I should’ve caught.\n\nThen it jumps to money, like whether my bills are going to start piling up if anything changes at work, or even if nothing changes and I’m just not managing things well enough. And then it turns into my family thinking I’m falling behind, or that I’m not doing enough with my life. And then grad school — like maybe I’ve already messed up my chances somehow, or I waited too long, or I haven’t done enough to be taken seriously.\n\nSo… yeah, it’s a lot of different things. It’s not like there’s one clear emergency I can point to. It’s more like everything feels like it could become a problem, and my mind keeps jumping between all of it most days.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you had little pleasure or interest in doing things, or felt down, depressed, or hopeless nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer the “nearly every day” part. I wouldn’t say I’ve been feeling hopeless like that every day, or that I just don’t care about anything anymore. It’s not that clean for me.\n\nThere are times where things don’t feel as enjoyable as they probably should, but I don’t know if that’s the same as having little interest or pleasure. Like, I may still want to do things, but I’m not always fully able to relax into them. So if you’re asking whether I’ve been depressed or hopeless almost every day for the last two weeks, I don’t think that’s really it. But if you’re asking whether I’ve felt kind of mentally weighed down or not fully present, then… maybe, yeah, somewhat.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt down, depressed, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy for most days?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m still not totally sure how to separate that from just being worn out and preoccupied. I haven’t really been tracking it that way. I notice more that I’m not as relaxed as I used to be, and that makes it harder to enjoy things fully, but I don’t think I’ve completely lost interest in them.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you had days where you felt a loss of pleasure or interest in most activities you usually enjoy, even things that normally make you happy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “most activities,” exactly. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F43.22, F33.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. I'm Dr. [Name]. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… um, I came in because my mind just won’t stop. It keeps jumping from one worry to another, and I can’t seem to get it to settle down. Like, I work in a lab, and there’s this new partnership happening, and I keep thinking, what if that means they’re going to cut my position? And then even if I try to focus on work, I start worrying that my data are wrong or that I missed something obvious and everyone’s going to find out.\n\nAnd then it kind of spirals into everything else—bills, whether they’re going to pile up, whether my family thinks I’m falling behind compared to where I should be, and whether I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school. It’s like my brain keeps moving through all these worst-case scenarios, one after another, and I can’t really shut it off.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries feel constant and uncontrollable, even when there isn’t a specific stressor happening, or do they mostly come up in response to certain situations or changes in your life?\n\nPatient: It feels pretty constant, honestly. Like, it definitely gets louder when something changes or there’s a specific thing happening, but it doesn’t need that to start. I can be trying to do something very normal, like finish a report or check an assay, and I’ll tell myself, okay, just focus on this one task, just get through this one thing. But then one worry sets off another one, and then another, and before I know it I’m just staring at the screen, not really reading what’s in front of me, because my head is already running through every possible way things could go wrong.\n\nAnd it’s not like I’m choosing to think about it. I’ll try to reason with myself, like, “You don’t have evidence for that,” or “You can deal with it later,” but it doesn’t really stop. It’s more like the worry just keeps branching out on its own, and I’m kind of stuck following it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms along with these worries, like feeling restless, being easily fatigued, having trouble sleeping, or muscle tension?\n\nPatient: Yeah, the restlessness is probably the biggest one I notice. At work especially, I feel kind of wound up, like I can’t just sit in my chair and do the thing in front of me. I’ll be trying to analyze something or write notes, and I’m tapping my foot the whole time, or shifting around, or checking my messages even when I know there’s probably nothing there.\n\nAnd I keep getting up for no real reason. Like I’ll walk back to the instrument room, and then once I’m there I’m like, why did I come in here? I don’t actually need to check anything right now. But sitting still feels… I don’t know, almost impossible sometimes. And I get jumpy too—normal lab noises, like something clicking on or someone closing a drawer, can startle me way more than they should. It’s embarrassing because it makes me feel like I’m on edge all day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, like hobbies or time with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I don’t think I’m, like, hopeless exactly. I wouldn’t say that’s the main thing. It’s more that I’m so caught up in my head that I don’t always have much room for other things, if that makes sense.\n\nLike with hobbies or seeing people, I don’t know that I’ve lost interest in them in the sense that I don’t care anymore. It’s more like when I’m doing them, I’m not fully there. I might go along with something or talk to family, but part of me is still kind of mentally elsewhere, thinking through things. So I guess I still want to enjoy stuff, and I do sometimes, but it can feel harder to actually settle into it.\n\nBut no, I don’t think I’ve been walking around feeling hopeless or like nothing matters. It’s more… tense and preoccupied, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you worry constantly, even about small or everyday things, and does it feel difficult to control this worry most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, most days it feels really hard to control. It’s not always about something big, either. Sometimes it’s something totally normal or small, like finishing a report, checking an assay, making sure I didn’t miss something obvious, and I’ll tell myself, just focus on this one task, just do the next step.\n\nBut then it’s like one worry opens the door to another one. I’ll think, what if I made a mistake here, and then it turns into what if that affects the results, and then what if someone notices, or what if I don’t catch it in time, and then suddenly I’m not even really doing the thing anymore. I’m just staring at the screen, kind of frozen, while my head is running through every possible way it could go wrong.\n\nAnd I do try to stop it. I’ll try to be logical with myself or redirect back to what I’m supposed to be doing, but it doesn’t feel like something I can just switch off. It keeps going even when I know it’s not helping.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you feel anxious or worried about a number of different things, not just specific problems, and that this anxiety is present more days than not?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It’s definitely not just one specific problem. That’s actually kind of why I came in, because it feels like my brain won’t stay on one worry long enough for me to even deal with it.\n\nLike, I’ll start with work — whether this new partnership our lab has is going to mean cuts, and whether my position is going to be one of the ones they decide they don’t need. And then before I’ve even finished thinking that through, I’m worried my data are wrong, or that I missed something in the analysis, or that someone’s going to find some mistake I should’ve caught.\n\nThen it jumps to money, like whether my bills are going to start piling up if anything changes at work, or even if nothing changes and I’m just not managing things well enough. And then it turns into my family thinking I’m falling behind, or that I’m not doing enough with my life. And then grad school — like maybe I’ve already messed up my chances somehow, or I waited too long, or I haven’t done enough to be taken seriously.\n\nSo… yeah, it’s a lot of different things. It’s not like there’s one clear emergency I can point to. It’s more like everything feels like it could become a problem, and my mind keeps jumping between all of it most days.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you had little pleasure or interest in doing things, or felt down, depressed, or hopeless nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer the “nearly every day” part. I wouldn’t say I’ve been feeling hopeless like that every day, or that I just don’t care about anything anymore. It’s not that clean for me.\n\nThere are times where things don’t feel as enjoyable as they probably should, but I don’t know if that’s the same as having little interest or pleasure. Like, I may still want to do things, but I’m not always fully able to relax into them. So if you’re asking whether I’ve been depressed or hopeless almost every day for the last two weeks, I don’t think that’s really it. But if you’re asking whether I’ve felt kind of mentally weighed down or not fully present, then… maybe, yeah, somewhat.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt down, depressed, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy for most days?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m still not totally sure how to separate that from just being worn out and preoccupied. Like, I wouldn’t say I wake up most days thinking, “I’m depressed,” or that I don’t enjoy anything anymore. It’s more that if I try to do something I usually like, part of me is still somewhere else, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… for most days, maybe I’ve felt kind of muted or distracted, but I don’t know if I’d call it feeling down or hopeless. I haven’t really been tracking it that way. I notice more that I’m not as relaxed as I used to be, and that makes it harder to enjoy things fully, but I don’t think I’ve completely lost interest in them.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you had days where you felt a loss of pleasure or interest in most activities you usually enjoy, even things that normally make you happy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “most activities,” exactly. That sounds stronger than what I’m sure about. There have definitely been days where things that usually feel good don’t really land the same way, like I’m doing them but not really getting the normal enjoyment out of them.\n\nBut I’m hesitant to say it’s a full loss of pleasure, because sometimes I still want to do those things, or I can tell that some part of me would enjoy them if I could just… settle into it. It’s more like the enjoyment feels dulled or blocked, not completely gone. And I’m not sure if that counts as losing interest, because the interest is still sort of there, it’s just harder to access.\n\nSo, yes, there have been days like that, but I don’t know if I can honestly say it’s been most activities or that even happy things don’t make me happy at all. It’s more inconsistent and kind of hard to separate from being distracted or preoccupied.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you experience excessive anxiety and worry on more days than not, even if the specific things you’re worrying about change from day to day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think that’s actually a better description of what’s been going on than the depression questions. It’s not always the exact same worry, but it feels like my brain just keeps swapping one thing out for another.\n\nLike, I came in because my mind is constantly jumping from whether our lab’s new partnership is going to mean my position gets cut, to whether the data I’ve been working with are wrong, to whether my bills are going to start piling up if something happens with work. Then it’ll shift to whether my family thinks I’m falling behind compared to where I should be, and then suddenly I’m thinking I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school somehow.\n\nIt’s not like I sit down and decide to worry about these things. It just keeps moving. Even if I talk myself down from one thing, another one kind of takes its place. So, more days than not, yeah, I’d say the worry is there. The topic changes, but the feeling doesn’t really stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you had several days in a row where you felt down or lost interest in things, and if so, did those days add up to two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I can say that, no. I mean, I’ve had stretches where I felt kind of flat or not very engaged, like I mentioned, but I don’t know that I’d call it several days in a row of being down or losing interest in things in the way you’re asking.\n\nIt hasn’t felt like a clear two-week block where I was depressed or couldn’t enjoy anything. It’s more scattered, or maybe it blends into the background enough that I have trouble separating it out. Some days I’ll notice I’m not getting much out of things, and then another day I might still enjoy something a little, or at least feel like I want to. So I don’t think those days add up cleanly to two weeks or more—not that I’ve noticed, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a clear change in your mood or ability to enjoy things compared to how you used to feel before this started?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I don’t know that I’ve noticed some clear, obvious change where I can point to “before” and “after” and say, yes, my mood or enjoyment is definitely different now.\n\nIt’s more… if there is a difference, it’s kind of hard for me to separate from everything else going on in my head. Like, I can still enjoy things sometimes, at least a little, and I don’t feel like I’ve become a completely different person emotionally. But I also don’t always feel as present with things as I used to, if that makes sense.\n\nI guess I’d say I haven’t noticed a clear change in the way you’re asking. Or at least not one I feel confident describing. It’s more vague than that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It’s more scattered, or maybe it blends into the background enough that I have trouble separating it out. Some days I’ll notice I’m not getting much out of things, and then another day I might still enjoy something a little, or at least feel like I want to. So I don’t think those days add up cleanly to two weeks or more—not that I’ve noticed, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a clear change in your mood or ability to enjoy things compared to how you used to feel before this started?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I don’t know that I’ve noticed some clear, obvious change where I can point to “before” and “after” and say, yes, my mood or enjoyment is definitely different now.\n\nIt’s more… if there is a difference, it’s kind of hard for me to separate from everything else going on in my head. Like, I can still enjoy things sometimes, at least a little, and I don’t feel like I’ve become a completely different person emotionally. But I also don’t always feel as present with things as I used to, if that makes sense.\n\nI guess I’d say I haven’t noticed a clear change in the way you’re asking. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think I kind of answered this before, but… no, I don’t think so. Not in a clean way.

I’ve had days where I felt off, or kind of muted, or like I wasn’t getting as much out of things as I normally would, but I don’t remember it being several days in a row where I was clearly down or clearly unable to enjoy things. And definitely not something I could add up and say, “That was two weeks.”

It’s more inconsistent than that. Like one day I might feel flat, then another day I can still get into something a little bit, or at least I’m not feeling actively down. So I’m not sure I’d describe it as a two-week stretch. If it happened, I didn’t recognize it that way.

Like, I work in a lab, and there’s this new partnership happening, and I keep thinking, what if that means they’re going to cut my position? And then even if I try to focus on work, I start worrying that my data are wrong or that I missed something obvious and everyone’s going to find out.\n\nAnd then it kind of spirals into everything else—bills, whether they’re going to pile up, whether my family thinks I’m falling behind compared to where I should be, and whether I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school. It’s like my brain keeps moving through all these worst-case scenarios, one after another, and I can’t really shut it off.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries feel constant and uncontrollable, even when there isn’t a specific stressor happening, or do they mostly come up in response to certain situations or changes in your life?\n\nPatient: It feels pretty constant, honestly. Like, it definitely gets louder when something changes or there’s a specific thing happening, but it doesn’t need that to start. I can be trying to do something very normal, like finish a report or check an assay, and I’ll tell myself, okay, just focus on this one task, just get through this one thing. But then one worry sets off another one, and then another, and before I know it I’m just staring at the screen, not really reading what’s in front of me, because my head is already running through every possible way things could go wrong.\n\nAnd it’s not like I’m choosing to think about it. I’ll try to reason with myself, like, “You don’t have evidence for that,” or “You can deal with it later,” but it doesn’t really stop. It’s more like the worry just keeps branching out on its own, and I’m kind of stuck following it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms along with these worries, like feeling restless, being easily fatigued, having trouble sleeping, or muscle tension?\n\nPatient: Yeah, the restlessness is probably the biggest one I notice. At work especially, I feel kind of wound up, like I can’t just sit in my chair and do the thing in front of me. I’ll be trying to analyze something or write notes, and I’m tapping my foot the whole time, or shifting around, or checking my messages even when I know there’s probably nothing there.\n\nAnd I keep getting up for no real reason. Like I’ll walk back to the instrument room, and then once I’m there I’m like, why did I come in here? I don’t actually need to check anything right now. But sitting still feels… I don’t know, almost impossible sometimes. And I get jumpy too—normal lab noises, like something clicking on or someone closing a drawer, can startle me way more than they should. It’s embarrassing because it makes me feel like I’m on edge all day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, like hobbies or time with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I don’t think I’m, like, hopeless exactly. I wouldn’t say that’s the main thing. It’s more that I’m so caught up in my head that I don’t always have much room for other things, if that makes sense.\n\nLike with hobbies or seeing people, I don’t know that I’ve lost interest in them in the sense that I don’t care anymore. It’s more like when I’m doing them, I’m not fully there. I might go along with something or talk to family, but part of me is still kind of mentally elsewhere, thinking through things. So I guess I still want to enjoy stuff, and I do sometimes, but it can feel harder to actually settle into it.\n\nBut no, I don’t think I’ve been walking around feeling hopeless or like nothing matters. It’s more… tense and preoccupied, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you worry constantly, even about small or everyday things, and does it feel difficult to control this worry most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, most days it feels really hard to control. It’s not always about something big, either. Sometimes it’s something totally normal or small, like finishing a report, checking an assay, making sure I didn’t miss something obvious, and I’ll tell myself, just focus on this one task, just do the next step.\n\nBut then it’s like one worry opens the door to another one. I’ll think, what if I made a mistake here, and then it turns into what if that affects the results, and then what if someone notices, or what if I don’t catch it in time, and then suddenly I’m not even really doing the thing anymore. I’m just staring at the screen, kind of frozen, while my head is running through every possible way it could go wrong.\n\nAnd I do try to stop it. I’ll try to be logical with myself or redirect back to what I’m supposed to be doing, but it doesn’t feel like something I can just switch off. It keeps going even when I know it’s not helping.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you feel anxious or worried about a number of different things, not just specific problems, and that this anxiety is present more days than not?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It’s definitely not just one specific problem. That’s actually kind of why I came in, because it feels like my brain won’t stay on one worry long enough for me to even deal with it.\n\nLike, I’ll start with work — whether this new partnership our lab has is going to mean cuts, and whether my position is going to be one of the ones they decide they don’t need. And then before I’ve even finished thinking that through, I’m worried my data are wrong, or that I missed something in the analysis, or that someone’s going to find some mistake I should’ve caught.\n\nThen it jumps to money, like whether my bills are going to start piling up if anything changes at work, or even if nothing changes and I’m just not managing things well enough. And then it turns into my family thinking I’m falling behind, or that I’m not doing enough with my life. And then grad school — like maybe I’ve already messed up my chances somehow, or I waited too long, or I haven’t done enough to be taken seriously.\n\nSo… yeah, it’s a lot of different things. It’s not like there’s one clear emergency I can point to. It’s more like everything feels like it could become a problem, and my mind keeps jumping between all of it most days.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you had little pleasure or interest in doing things, or felt down, depressed, or hopeless nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer the “nearly every day” part. I wouldn’t say I’ve been feeling hopeless like that every day, or that I just don’t care about anything anymore. It’s not that clean for me.\n\nThere are times where things don’t feel as enjoyable as they probably should, but I don’t know if that’s the same as having little interest or pleasure. Like, I may still want to do things, but I’m not always fully able to relax into them. So if you’re asking whether I’ve been depressed or hopeless almost every day for the last two weeks, I don’t think that’s really it. But if you’re asking whether I’ve felt kind of mentally weighed down or not fully present, then… maybe, yeah, somewhat.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt down, depressed, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy for most days?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m still not totally sure how to separate that from just being worn out and preoccupied. It’s more that if I try to do something I usually like, part of me is still somewhere else, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… for most days, maybe I’ve felt kind of muted or distracted, but I don’t know if I’d call it feeling down or hopeless. I haven’t really been tracking it that way. I notice more that I’m not as relaxed as I used to be, and that makes it harder to enjoy things fully, but I don’t think I’ve completely lost interest in them.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you had days where you felt a loss of pleasure or interest in most activities you usually enjoy, even things that normally make you happy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “most activities,” exactly. That sounds stronger than what I’m sure about. There have definitely been days where things that usually feel good don’t really land the same way, like I’m doing them but not really getting the normal enjoyment out of them.\n\nBut I’m hesitant to say it’s a full loss of pleasure, because sometimes I still want to do those things, or I can tell that some part of me would enjoy them if I could just… settle into it. And then before I’ve even finished thinking that through, I’m worried my data are wrong, or that I missed something in the analysis, or that someone’s going to find some mistake I should’ve caught.\n\nThen it jumps to money, like whether my bills are going to start piling up if anything changes at work, or even if nothing changes and I’m just not managing things well enough. And then it turns into my family thinking I’m falling behind, or that I’m not doing enough with my life. And then grad school — like maybe I’ve already messed up my chances somehow, or I waited too long, or I haven’t done enough to be taken seriously.\n\nSo… yeah, it’s a lot of different things. It’s not like there’s one clear emergency I can point to. It’s more like everything feels like it could become a problem, and my mind keeps jumping between all of it most days.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you had little pleasure or interest in doing things, or felt down, depressed, or hopeless nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer the “nearly every day” part. I wouldn’t say I’ve been feeling hopeless like that every day, or that I just don’t care about anything anymore. It’s not that clean for me.\n\nThere are times where things don’t feel as enjoyable as they probably should, but I don’t know if that’s the same as having little interest or pleasure. Like, I may still want to do things, but I’m not always fully able to relax into them. So if you’re asking whether I’ve been depressed or hopeless almost every day for the last two weeks, I don’t think that’s really it. But if you’re asking whether I’ve felt kind of mentally weighed down or not fully present, then… maybe, yeah, somewhat.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt down, depressed, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy for most days?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m still not totally sure how to separate that from just being worn out and preoccupied. Like, I wouldn’t say I wake up most days thinking, “I’m depressed,” or that I don’t enjoy anything anymore. It’s more that if I try to do something I usually like, part of me is still somewhere else, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… for most days, maybe I’ve felt kind of muted or distracted, but I don’t know if I’d call it feeling down or hopeless. I haven’t really been tracking it that way. I notice more that I’m not as relaxed as I used to be, and that makes it harder to enjoy things fully, but I don’t think I’ve completely lost interest in them.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you had days where you felt a loss of pleasure or interest in most activities you usually enjoy, even things that normally make you happy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “most activities,” exactly. That sounds stronger than what I’m sure about. There have definitely been days where things that usually feel good don’t really land the same way, like I’m doing them but not really getting the normal enjoyment out of them.\n\nBut I’m hesitant to say it’s a full loss of pleasure, because sometimes I still want to do those things, or I can tell that some part of me would enjoy them if I could just… settle into it. It’s more like the enjoyment feels dulled or blocked, not completely gone. And I’m not sure if that counts as losing interest, because the interest is still sort of there, it’s just harder to access.\n\nSo, yes, there have been days like that, but I don’t know if I can honestly say it’s been most activities or that even happy things don’t make me happy at all. It’s more inconsistent and kind of hard to separate from being distracted or preoccupied.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you experience excessive anxiety and worry on more days than not, even if the specific things you’re worrying about change from day to day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think that’s actually a better description of what’s been going on than the depression questions. I haven’t really been tracking it that way. I notice more that I’m not as relaxed as I used to be, and that makes it harder to enjoy things fully, but I don’t think I’ve completely lost interest in them.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you had days where you felt a loss of pleasure or interest in most activities you usually enjoy, even things that normally make you happy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “most activities,” exactly. That sounds stronger than what I’m sure about. There have definitely been days where things that usually feel good don’t really land the same way, like I’m doing them but not really getting the normal enjoyment out of them.\n\nBut I’m hesitant to say it’s a full loss of pleasure, because sometimes I still want to do those things, or I can tell that some part of me would enjoy them if I could just… settle into it. It’s more like the enjoyment feels dulled or blocked, not completely gone. And I’m not sure if that counts as losing interest, because the interest is still sort of there, it’s just harder to access.\n\nSo, yes, there have been days like that, but I don’t know if I can honestly say it’s been most activities or that even happy things don’t make me happy at all. It’s more inconsistent and kind of hard to separate from being distracted or preoccupied.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you experience excessive anxiety and worry on more days than not, even if the specific things you’re worrying about change from day to day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think that’s actually a better description of what’s been going on than the depression questions. It’s not always the exact same worry, but it feels like my brain just keeps swapping one thing out for another.\n\nLike, I came in because my mind is constantly jumping from whether our lab’s new partnership is going to mean my position gets cut, to whether the data I’ve been working with are wrong, to whether my bills are going to start piling up if something happens with work. Then it’ll shift to whether my family thinks I’m falling behind compared to where I should be, and then suddenly I’m thinking I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school somehow.\n\nIt’s not like I sit down and decide to worry about these things. It just keeps moving. Even if I talk myself down from one thing, another one kind of takes its place. So, more days than not, yeah, I’d say the worry is there. The topic changes, but the feeling doesn’t really stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you had several days in a row where you felt down or lost interest in things, and if so, did those days add up to two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I can say that, no. I mean, I’ve had stretches where I felt kind of flat or not very engaged, like I mentioned, but I don’t know that I’d call it several days in a row of being down or losing interest in things in the way you’re asking.\n\nIt hasn’t felt like a clear two-week block where I was depressed or couldn’t enjoy anything. It’s more scattered, or maybe it blends into the background enough that I have trouble separating it out. Some days I’ll notice I’m not getting much out of things, and then another day I might still enjoy something a little, or at least feel like I want to. So I don’t think those days add up cleanly to two weeks or more—not that I’ve noticed, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a clear change in your mood or ability to enjoy things compared to how you used to feel before this started?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I don’t know that I’ve noticed some clear, obvious change where I can point to “before” and “after” and say, yes, my mood or enjoyment is definitely different now.\n\nIt’s more… if there is a difference, it’s kind of hard for me to separate from everything else going on in my head. I don’t know that I’ve noticed some clear, obvious change where I can point to “before” and “after” and say, yes, my mood or enjoyment is definitely different now.\n\nIt’s more… if there is a difference, it’s kind of hard for me to separate from everything else going on in my head. Like, I can still enjoy things sometimes, at least a little, and I don’t feel like I’ve become a completely different person emotionally. But I also don’t always feel as present with things as I used to, if that makes sense.\n\nI guess I’d say I haven’t noticed a clear change in the way you’re asking. Or at least not one I feel confident describing. It’s more vague than that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had several days in a row where you felt down or lost interest in things, and if so, did those days add up to two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: I think I kind of answered this before, but… no, I don’t think so. Not in a clean way.\n\nI’ve had days where I felt off, or kind of muted, or like I wasn’t getting as much out of things as I normally would, but I don’t remember it being several days in a row where I was clearly down or clearly unable to enjoy things. And definitely not something I could add up and say, “That was two weeks.”\n\nIt’s more inconsistent than that. Like one day I might feel flat, then another day I can still get into something a little bit, or at least I’m not feeling actively down. So I’m not sure I’d describe it as a two-week stretch. If it happened, I didn’t recognize it that way.\n\nDoctor: Over the past few weeks, have you had days where you felt down or low most of the time, even if it wasn’t severe or didn’t stop you from doing your usual activities?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not in the sense of being low most of the time for a whole day. I mean, I’ve definitely had moments where I feel kind of heavy or discouraged, but when you say “down or low most of the time,” that makes me hesitate, because I’m not sure I’d describe it that way.\n\nOver the past few weeks, it’s been more like my mood shifts around, or I’ll have patches where I’m not feeling great, and then I’m distracted by something or I’m just going through the day and I don’t notice it as much. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m still kind of unsure how to answer that. I know you’re asking if there have been several days where it was most of the day, and I don’t think I can clearly say yes to that.

I’ve had periods during the day where I feel sort of disconnected from enjoying things, or like I’m not really getting much out of them, but I don’t know that it lasts most of the day. It’s more like I’ll notice it for a while, then I get pulled into whatever I’m doing, or I’m just distracted, and I’m not really checking in with myself enough to say, “Okay, I was down all day.”

So… no, I don’t think I’ve had several days like that in the way you mean. Or at least I haven’t noticed it clearly enough to say that. It’s more scattered and hard to pin down than a full-day thing.

I’ll try to be logical with myself or redirect back to what I’m supposed to be doing, but it doesn’t feel like something I can just switch off. It keeps going even when I know it’s not helping.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you feel anxious or worried about a number of different things, not just specific problems, and that this anxiety is present more days than not?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It’s definitely not just one specific problem. That’s actually kind of why I came in, because it feels like my brain won’t stay on one worry long enough for me to even deal with it.\n\nLike, I’ll start with work — whether this new partnership our lab has is going to mean cuts, and whether my position is going to be one of the ones they decide they don’t need. And then before I’ve even finished thinking that through, I’m worried my data are wrong, or that I missed something in the analysis, or that someone’s going to find some mistake I should’ve caught.\n\nThen it jumps to money, like whether my bills are going to start piling up if anything changes at work, or even if nothing changes and I’m just not managing things well enough. I wouldn’t say I’ve been feeling hopeless like that every day, or that I just don’t care about anything anymore.
